So first I wanted to talk a little bit about, um, how GrokBot came to be. And, um,
I think we're in a pretty interesting moment right now where, when you think about the way that AI
has changed and really transformed the way that, that people and companies do work,
I think we've been through a few different phases. And I think it started with really chat,
where you ask a question to AI, AI responds. Uh, and that was, that was really useful. I think
that helped with a bunch of research workflows, for example. I think the next step after that was
you delegate a task to AI and there's a copilot that kind of sits next to you while you do your work.
You iterate on a slide deck together, you know, those types of, of workflows. Uh, but I think we're
really on the cusp of this new moment in AI, uh, where AI starts to act more like a teammate or more like
a colleague inside of a company. And that's really different, I think, from what's come before. And I think
it's going to change the types of products that really meet the moment. I think the ways that we need to
change our ways and how we do work day to day to really maximally get the most out of, out of what AI
can do inside of a company. And then I think what will come after that, um, is kind of this weird world of
AI teammates and human teammates that collaborate together inside of companies. And where you're not just
delegating a discrete task and saying, Hey, go from point A to point B, you're actually doing a lot more
kind of what you would do with, with an actual human colleague of they're not a collection of tasks
that you kind of outsource, but they own outcomes and they have responsibilities and you trust them to
kind of own a discrete part of a company, um, and, and kind of do that, do that work for you. Um, and I think this is
really where GrokBot originated from and I want to talk a little bit about some of the product decisions
that we made to, to get there. So if you haven't used GrokBot, this is what it looks like. And I
think there were a few things that we chose to do with GrokBot that are a bit different from other AI
tools on the market, um, that you've maybe have have used before or familiar with. I think the first
one is we really wanted GrokBot to feel a lot like you're working with teammates and colleagues. And so
as a result, it feels kind of simple and chat-like and almost like you're messaging, um, you know,
your coworkers or teams of colleagues. So you have on the left all of these bots, in this case,
sales outbound and an inbox manager that each have specific roles, um, and different kind of
lanes of work. And the idea is not that you create a chat for each task that you want to take on. You
actually create a bot for a unit of work and a kind of job responsibility and you come back to that bot
over and over and over again and it learns from you. And I think that's one of the most magical
moments of working with GrokBot is you say something a few weeks ago about, you know,
I really like when my slides are formatted in this particular way. And then GrokBot
remembers. It remembers weeks later when you're making your next slide deck. And that's what you
would expect from a colleague. And I think that's what you should expect from AI as well. So there's
this teammate paradigm, uh, instead of a task paradigm. Teammates really sit at the center of, of GrokBot
and the way to work with AI. The second thing that's a little bit different, um, is GrokBot,
similar to what you would expect from a colleague, um, doesn't just have access to direct integrations
and MCPs and APIs. Those are very useful, but they oftentimes don't get you a hundred percent of the
way to completing the work that you want. They have access to their own computer and they can use that
computer exactly how you would. They can watch a video, they can listen to a podcast, they can click in
some, you know, uh, antiquated, uh, government software from, from the nineties. Um, it can do
everything that a human can do with a computer. Um, but it's yours. And in addition to all of the
integrations and MCPs and things that you come to, to know and love with AI, I think it really opens up
a new set of, of tasks and opportunities for what AI can take off of your plate. And I think it feels
really different when you work with AI that can do those things versus can't. And the difference
between getting kind of 90% of the way there of like almost having the job be done versus the thing
just being done end to end, kind of hands off, you trust that your bot can do it, feels very different,
feels really magical. And then the last thing is everything runs in the cloud. It doesn't run on your
computer. And I think there's, we're kind of in this weird moment in AI right now where a lot of
the workflows that people rely so much on AI for, it runs on their local laptop, which is kind of
strange. And so if you're like asleep and your computer's closed, you know, the automation that
you set up to run at 2am doesn't fire, uh, and you wake up in the morning and you're confused and
you want to kick something off from your phone, but it's tethered to your laptop back in your office.
It's just all this weirdness. And we wanted to just jump right past that to a future where you
have these very smart colleagues, these, these teammates, these bots that live in the cloud,
and you can interact with them anywhere you work. It might be on your phone, it might be on your
laptop. In the future, it might be something entirely different. And that really informed all of the
decisions we made for the product was those three things. It feels like interacting with teammates,
everything runs in the cloud, and your bots have access to a computer that they can drive and
manipulate just like you can. And I think part of the reason we really felt the the desire and the
excitement to build something like Rockbot is from what we've seen in coding. And I think from the
past two years, we've seen the job of a developer completely transform. And I think a very stark,
it's hard to overestimate how big, how big the change has been, um, from an era of you're writing
individual lines of code by hand to, I think, what a lot of development looks like now, certainly inside
of SpaceX AI, where you have developers that own outcomes, they own direction, and they steer their
team of agents to complete these discrete tasks and do kind of a lot of the mundane work for them. And
we haven't seen that for knowledge work at all. I think when you look at the way that knowledge work
is done today versus the way it's done in 2024 or 2025, it's not that different. And I think our
hope and goal for Rockbot is to really be the most useful way to harness these models inside of a company to do real work.
And so I touched on some of these points, but Amrita will talk a little bit more about specific
ways that we're seeing Rockbot be useful for us internally, but also inside of real companies in the wild.
But we wanted it to be delightfully simple. And we think the future of AI products
really should be very simple to use, very simple to get started on, and then gradually pull you into the more complex
capabilities. And I think a lot of people's experience using Rockbot is you meet it on its face,
you kind of give it an initial task that is a little straightforward and simple,
Rockbot does it, you give it something more ambitious, Rockbot does it, you give it something even more ambitious,
And you just kind of keep going through that process until you you feel the limits of maybe where current AI capabilities are.
And our bet is, you know, these models are going to keep getting smarter.
We want to give them a safe environment to run in to really maximally be useful to employees of companies.
So it's very simple. It's always on, you know, it's this this this teammate in the cloud that you can always depend on and rely on and
never goes down even when you're asleep or when your computer is closed and it really finishes the work.
You give it outcomes, it delivers. And I think the next step of this will be how this works inside of teams.
I think right now, AI is largely a single player game, you know, you work with your personal AI assistant on various things.
I think we're really excited about what shared AI colleagues could look like and what the future of internal tooling even becomes.
So with that, very excited to pass it off to Amrita to talk a bit more about concrete ways that people are using Rockbot and ways that we're using Rockbot.

in kind of the GrokBot for engineering and GrokBot for sales demos is those agents are long lived
personas. They have memory. And so because of that, they're not just scoped to one task or one project,
they can be used for multiple tasks and multiple projects. I can have, you know, email Ethan work for
the next year if I wanted. And now he will have context about every email that I've sent. And he'll
be able to learn from all of that. Similarly, slide Sonia, she doesn't know just how to make, you know,
this one type of slide. I can teach her how to work in, you know, a different branding or a different
theme. Her job is just to create slides generally. And so I can always change the scope and the persona
of these agents in order to make them as useful to me as possible. The way I like to think about
GrokBot generally is you're basically creating your own company. You're creating your own team of
agents and you have the choice and the decision of how you want to orchestrate that. If you want your
agents to be divided up by, you know, project, you can do that. If you want them to be divided up by
what they're expert in, you can do that as well. And so it's kind of up to you and how you want to

Hi. Thank you for the presentation. Can you explain a little bit how the memory works?
Yeah.
Can you edit it? Can you curate it? How does it forget stuff that you don't need anymore?
Great question. So memory is stored in S3 buckets inside of GrokBot and basically what that means is it's long lived, it's persistent, but you can also modify it.
And so if there's anything that you want to do around, you know, asking it to forget a particular thing that it's done.
For example, one time I had a list of bots that I deleted for a particular demo and I asked, you know, the chief of staff bot to say,
hey, you know, remember those two bots that you spun up? I want you to no longer delegate to them or use them.
And so there's kind of a little bit of a nuance there where a bot has memory about everything that you've told it.
But for example, if it delegated to another bot that no longer exists, it's not going to remember that or it's not going to know that that bot has been deleted.
So there are some things there where you're going to have to update and kind of improve its memory so that it's able to learn from those things.
Another great thing about memory is, as I mentioned, you can duplicate bots.
When you duplicate a bot, what's nice about it is that it starts with fresh context and fresh memory.
And so it'll have the same kind of persona that you want, which is, for example, this email Ethan,
but it's not going to have any of the memory that it had before.
It's almost like starting like a brand new, you know, human or robot with zero memory,
but still the workings and understandings of how to go forward.
Great question.
Okay, we have about four more minutes.
I'm going to put this slide back up so you all can see it.
And then I will end with just a couple learnings to kind of sum up what we've done so far.
My biggest advice about GrokBot is to try it.
It's very simple, as you saw, to spin up any number of bots.
But I think what I like to do whenever I think about using GrokBot is I think of
what is the most, you know, annoying part of my day that I wish I didn't have to do.
In this scenario, it could be, you know, creating Google forms and sending them out
and then analyzing data.
It could be something from, you know, outbounding emails.
You'll see a lot of that in the GrokBot for sales and GrokBot for SDRs.
It could be something, like, for the engineering team, like cleaning up stale feature flags.
For field engineers, it could be anything from responding to, like, customer support tickets
that I've, you know, forgotten about.
So I encourage everyone to think about a part of their day that they wish they didn't have to do
or they wish they could get time back from.
And then work with GrokBot on it.
Think of some agents that you can work on, whether you give them kind of a personality
and the kind of workings to be able to do your job, but also to take that part of your job
and then invest that time elsewhere.
I think that's the beauty of GrokBot is now I have this time back that I can spend either,
you know, curating more demos or drinking more coffee.
Whatever kind of makes me happy is what I'm able to do thanks to GrokBot.

To summarize today's learnings, kind of three main things.
The first is bots can access tools that are outside of MCPs.
So bots can create forums.
Bots can create Qualtrics data.
Bots can, again, use services inside of your guys' customer, you know, VPNs.
What's nice about that is that you no longer have to connect to MCPs and go through auth.
All you have to do is give bot its computer and say,
"Hey, this is what I want you to access.
This is what I want you to do."
Again, start with the outcome with bot,
and then it'll be able to kind of work backwards, as I said,
and figure out what it needs to do in order to get to that outcome.
Bots have memory and can learn from video teaching.
So we saw that earlier where I taught bot how to learn animation.
Then I was able to kind of use that as a skill and apply that going forward.
We talked about memory a little bit in terms of you can have basically a bot that works across
multiple projects because it's an expert in its domain.
It's a persona.
It's not a task.

many different things.

So, let me introduce you to the grogbots.
This is a team of fully autonomous AI agents designed for all types of works, including engineering.
First of all, it runs 24x7, and it is fully autonomous.
It can run on its own.
It is not limited to when your computer is asleep or not.
And it also does not require you to actually have a laptop.
The second thing is, which is the biggest unblock for the engineers, is it can manage coding agents for you.
Not only cursor cloud agents, it can almost manage any type of coding agents for you.
And the best thing is, it has the first-party integration with cursor cloud agents.
So, it has the tools to be able to read the transcripts to start a cursor cloud agent.
It can also start on a private worker, so that if you have your Mac machines you want to learn, it can start into the Mac machine.
And anything you can do in the cursor window with cloud agents, grogbots can do for you with tool calls.
And so, you don't have to, you know, let your grogbots control some user interface to start the agents and read the transcript.
It can efficiently read all these through their tool calls.
And to me, this unblocks a lot of workflows because it can examine what's being done by the cloud agents.

For example, like screenshots or the performance comparison before versus after.

So with that, we thought we'd spend just a second talking about the motivation behind rock bot.
In some ways, rock bot is the product we've also made.
And so there's a story to it having been built just the same way there's a story to every product having been built.
And, um, I think if you think back to sort of what motivated rock bot in the early, early days, it was realizing that, you know, at least internally within SpaceX AI, there are so many folks who were starting to evolve beyond a chat box.
And I think there's been a lot of discourse about this.
Maybe some of you all have felt this or read about it as well.
Um, but really it started to feel increasingly that agents weren't quite as home in a chat box as you might have wanted them to be.
I think a lot of this was just a natural evolution of how we started interacting with these large language models and sort of, you know, going from asking for a response and maybe waiting for some text to asking agents to drive outcomes.
And so obviously we saw a lot of this outcome-based agentic development happen when it comes to things like coding agents.
But, you know, as more and more of us internally started thinking about that beyond coding, it felt like the chat box was a little bit limiting.
And so we started exploring, you know, all kinds of ways that we might want to chat and work with agents and have them complete tasks and have access to systems that really go beyond the chat box.
And, you know, the most common thing that kept coming up in all of these conversations was that we kept sort of treating our agents as if they were colleagues.
We wanted to be able to interact with them as if they were sort of these smart colleagues that sat next to us at our desks or were messageable in Slack or had access to Linear to look at tickets or, you know, wrote PRDs with us in Notion or whatever it is.
And so a lot of the motivation behind how RockBot was evolved and kind of why it is the way it is today is grounded in this idea that, you know, we should really have these agentic colleagues that we can work with on all the great things we do when we build products.
And so that was really the motivation. So really, I think there's four things that we kind of took as a brief based on that colleague definition.
The first one is that colleagues, when you work with them, often tie multiple tools together.
They don't live in one system. They work across many systems. And those systems do different things, right?
So you might have a ticketing system, like I mentioned, Linear or Jira or something like it.
You might be using, you know, CRM or CRM++, you know, something like Salesforce or more.
You might be keeping documents in Notion, designs in Figma. And all these tools are really, really great at what they do.
But oftentimes your colleagues or, you know, yourselves are tying things together across them.
The other things here are that these colleagues often have long running context.
They learn and they don't just, you know, work on a task, eject the task and then move on.
The best colleagues you work with, the best teammates you work with, they learn on the job, right?
Maybe they start out with very little company context. You teach them some tasks or teach them how to get some work done.
They kind of do some work. You give them some feedback, right? That wasn't that great.
They hopefully take the feedback and then get better at their jobs.
We think that model generalizes to agents too, where agents can really, you know, the way that the models have evolved,
get good at learning, at sort of building memory for themselves, at realizing what's important, and at taking and incorporating that kind of feedback.
The third thing, and I think this is a really big one, is independence.
And I think for a lot of-- whoops, we lost the slides.
Let's go fix that. There we go.
For a lot of, you know, people we work with, they go execute things independently, right?
Once in a while maybe you'll get asked for, hey, I need some help on blocking a task.
Or, you know, maybe someone gets stuck on, you know, stuck with access to a system or something like this.
But really, the defining property of a colleague that you work with, one of the defining properties is that they can work on their own,
they can complete tasks on their own, and they have their own access.
And so this is really what motivated the need for GrokBot to have its own computer.
And so we decided, GrokBot, you know, should have its own computer that it can do things in.
And this is true, and this is how the product works today.
So if you go in and use GrokBot, GrokBot has its own computer.
You can kind of help it when it gets stuck on that computer.
But it has its own surface where it can create the output that you need to get a system in your work.
And then the last and final thing I'll call out here is messaging.
So earlier I mentioned that, you know, it felt like we hadn't, you know, we wanted to move beyond the chat box a little bit.
But I meant that a little bit tongue-in-cheek.
You know, the sort of chat box that defines how you interact with a large language of a model,
we think that that core premise makes still a lot of sense.
But where it started to feel a little bit constrained is that usually when you're chatting with a colleague,
you're like rapid-fire messaging.
If you're like me in Slack or maybe like us in Slack, you're constantly streaming out messages.
You're pushing between threads.
You're multitasking.
And we found that, you know, rather than having to wait your turn for the next response,
that it's a lot more engaging if you can just really jump in, interrupt, drive, and interact with agents in this way.
You'll see a little bit of this in a minute.
But really, I think those are the four things that we found as we kind of built this product and play with it internally
that, you know, define how colleagues work.

And I think to that point, you know, what we've seen a lot of folks do is they create many agents.
And so I wanted to spend a minute just talking about why we think that's effective.
One is that I think, you know, while agents might have massive context windows that can store entire novels, our brains or at least my brain can't.
And so oftentimes we just want to know who to reference.
And it feels like a really natural pattern to assign roles to these agents that correspond to how you'd interact with colleagues, as we've talked about.
So having a person who's in charge of data, maybe they do the job of five data people or assist with five data types of tasks in your organization,
can be really helpful for you invoking and working with those agents to know their role.
And so I find this really helpful just to know, you know, which bot I want to talk to for a given task.
And then sometimes their roles merge and that's okay.
We'll show you this.
But bots can also talk to each other, which is really cool.
And that can kind of coordinate in channels and stuff.
So we'll show that as an example too.
But the other reasons for having more than just one agent is that the bots do perform better when they can scope their memory down a little bit more.
Just going back to that idea of continuous learning and evolution.
Kind of that learning loop we found functions a lot better when a bot is sort of typed or cast into a specific role.
And then the final thing is, I think it's a little more fun and a little easier to parallelize your work.
But you can kind of hand stuff off to a bunch of bots.
And so one pattern that we see as fun internally is folks who will like fire off messages to six different bots.
You'll see them all spinning and working.
And they kind of all go and do some work for you and kind of come back and synthesize some results.
So, again, sort of mirroring how you might work, you know, with colleagues.
Let's say we're in a meeting together.
We assign a bunch of action items.
We all go off and do that work and then come back together and synthesize, say, a plan.
The bots can really mimic that behavior.
And I think it's also easier for us as humans to kind of know how we're steering them into that.

And I think what's really cool about this too is you can sort of choose how much human you want to bring into the loop, right?
So maybe for some of these cases, if it's a really easy change to say a documentation website or maybe it's resizing something in our mobile app, it feels like a pretty low stakes change and sometimes we can just let our agents run with it.
But perhaps there's a more meaningful change if we want to make sure that this design was implemented the way that we spec'd it out or the way that Pixel helped us spec'd it out.
We might want to jump in and ask for a demo or a prototype.
And so one thing we found that's just generally true when working with agents is that giving them an environment to verify their own output in makes a huge difference.
But also giving them the tools to help you as a human verify the output also makes a huge difference in outcomes and you can kind of work with those things together and get better results.
Amazing. So I think our engineering team is going to cook on this in the background.
And so what have we seen so far?
We started with this open question about the business.
We started talking to a data science colleague, Ashley, to help us better understand some of the funnel dynamics where the biggest fall off was.
Handed all of that context over to a PMP agent who could actually help write the product requirements doc.
That was the first pass.
We then handed it off to our designer who created a first version of the mock to actually go and implement.
And then Emily and our engineering team are off now cooking it with our cloud agents.
Really cool system. And again, you can really adapt this to whatever workflow makes the most sense to your business.
And so we've seen a lot of folks who want to go really deep on the requirements phase and maybe work on really complex systems where there's lots of knock-on implications for every small requirement.
You could imagine having a team of folks whose job it is to make sure there's a knowledge base that's kept up to date and that those requirements are fed into the product.
Or maybe you have a really convoluted code base with a bunch of different repos that need to stay in sync.
That's another workflow that you could build in with GrockBot.
And so my point with that is just stating that this is one example of how you might set up your team, but really the tool is meant to be flexible for you to kind of adapt to whatever workflow works for your own organization.
Okay, now a few of the hard-learned lessons working with GrockBot.
A few things to keep in mind as you start to build out your own teams in GrockBot.
First is this idea of named agents, having separate memory, being able to learn on the job, and really thinking about these as colleagues.
They're not going to be great on day zero.
There is an onboarding phase where you can give them skills, you can give them context, you can teach them things.
And so over time they actually build up that context and intuition and take on more and more work, which is cool to see.
The other is really thinking about sort of reducing noise in the system as PMs.
Like there is often a lot of pings, a lot of emails, a lot of slacks.
One of the pro tips that we've learned is when you set up a routine to ask your agent if this is essentially a no operation, if there's nothing important or urgent for me to see, you can actually just take the work on yourself or you don't need to update me.
And so there is a bit of noise reduction in the system.
Agents are in GrockBot is currently grooming my inbox and will only tell me about the most important emails there and can escalate things.
But generally I'm trying to reduce the noise in the system so I can keep focus on what is most important for the team.
And then agents all the way down, this idea that, you know, you can have managers of agents.
Agents are coordinating with cloud agents.
And GrockBot is a very helpful agent system for helping you take on some of the most ambitious work and accelerate your product.

So, from the Joy T, if friends can use each other's bots, do they inherit the bot's previous training?

So, it's interesting, currently, the way these bots work.
Like, if Lauren sent me a bot, that would be a template.
That template basically allows you to copy the memories, some of the context, or what the bots worked on, or, like, the instructions, as well as, like, first-party plugins and some other things, without carrying over sensitive data or, like, chat data.
So, if you shared a template with me, you wouldn't have to worry about sending me any passwords, or, number one, the bot can't see passwords.
We'll talk a little bit more about that in a second, but any sort of chat information that's specific to you.
Related, bots store passwords securely using, like, secure entries.
So, you actually don't need to worry about bots having access to any sensitive data.
You'll get, like, a nice little form.
And so, basically, the answer to that is, when you get a bot template, you're going to be able to refine it as you go.

I think Protobot is still going.
It's kicking off the Cloud Agent, and hopefully it finishes sometime soon.

And it can drive it and QA it.
And as these tests happen, it's very easily verifiable whether it's shipped the right thing or not.
And so I think all of these bots are very powerful, and most importantly, they're all powerful in conjunction.
So when CloseBot gets some feedback, it can pass it to Protobot.
Or when ProdBot is trying to get across some of the key product decisions that we've made, it can pass that context off to CloseBot so that it can sell the right stuff.
And overall, I think the key framework that you folks need to keep in mind is you want to start to try and productize yourself as much as possible and give away all of your Legos, no matter how delightful they are, so that you can focus on the highest leverage thing.
Because that is what helps you win in a world where everyone is shipping just as fast, if not faster, for now.
So keep all that in mind.

Okay.
Some final pieces.
What we learned.
Let your bots run free.
I am going to say this forever.
Give them access to as much as you are possibly comfortable with, because that is the only way they will be able to do work end-to-end.
You want to invest in them, so you might find times where you need to course-correct a bot.
And with an agent, you might have the instinct of, man, I spent too many tokens on course-correcting it.
The context is polluted.
Let me just get rid of it and start a new one.
In this case, your key advantage is that your bots get really good as you spend more time with them.
And obviously, as the team has learned internally, the more time you spend, the better you do.
And so it's really important to really believe in your bots the same way you would with employees.
And the other piece to keep in mind here is the other way to invest in them is by giving them really good skills and tools.
So it's very easy to say, hey, make this cooler.
And that could be your prompt that you want your bot to execute on.
But obviously, it's not going to do a great job.
It's going to probably get stuck somewhere and ask you a few questions.
The more intentionality you put behind that task in the first place lets you repeat it over and over again without that many hitches.
And then finally, it is worth you taking one to two hours and just thinking about your day.
I know that sounds dumb.
I know you're like, I need to move quickly.
But it is so, so important to just take the time.
I don't care when.
Just do it and figure out what you want to delegate.
Because doing that intentionally will save you so many hours versus just spinning up bots and hoping they do things for you without having a true intent around what they should do.
Okay, now I have a bonus round, some tips and tricks.
I was watching the live streamer earlier.
Some people were like, this is expensive, which is true in some ways, not true in others, and we're working on it.
But there are some really easy, high-leverage tips that can make your life a lot easier.
The first is that browser use is super powerful, and it unblocks your bot from doing a lot of things that it couldn't do if it didn't have MCPs or APIs or some other connection.
But it's also expensive to run multiple times.
And the easiest way to solve for this is to bias towards using APIs and using interfaces that people have already built, or to actually kind of hack it a little bit.
You can ask your bot to look at network requests while it does a task once, and then figure out which APIs are being hit while it's doing that task, and then go and start to just hit those directly, which takes way less time, first of all, and is also way more token efficient, because you are no longer needing to go through the browser interface, and you can just do it all directly.
So ask your bot, and this is true for all optimizations, by the way, that you guys have probably been thinking of or taking notes on.
Always just ask your bot how to do it, because it will figure it out for you, which is very nice.
But be wary of browser use more than once, and if you can get around it and scale quickly, you absolutely should.
Second piece, routines.
When I was looking through some of the data around the frequency of routines that people set, I was a little shocked, and it freaks me out how often you guys are running things.
But I think the most important part about routines is to audit them.
And you don't have to do this yourself.
Again, get a bot to do it for you, but the most important piece is be very intentional about how often your routines are running.
They're really powerful, but most people over-index on how often they need to be running, and they think, this routine is so important, I need to run it every 15 minutes, which comes out to 100 times a day.
So that is a very expensive routine.
See if you can be a little more nifty with them.
So it's not just running them fewer times.
If you really need a routine, try and run them on webhooks, or try and run them with some sort of inbound signal that will let your bots actually take in an input and then run it, rather than just having it blindly run on a schedule.
So those two things alone will probably save you a bunch in tokens.
I know there was some demand for that.
Okay.
Even more final tip before they cane me off stage.
Make a voice bot.
Obviously, I love mine.
Please do this for yourself.
It is the most underrated way to get words out to other people.
It's have a bot that copies your voice.
Import your cookies.
This lets you stay signed in across a lot of different tools.
And it also stops you from having to constantly take over the bot's computer and help out.
Group bots by expertise.
This is kind of a personal preference thing, but I love having my bots grouped by what they do.
And so if a bot is really good at just handling a customer, I will give it everything customer related.
And if a bot is really good at data analysis, that will be its sole job.
Because sometimes there are weird compounding pieces that happen that we don't even know about.
And so when you're giving it to what feel like completely different tasks, you can still get some of the compounding benefits as long as they all fall under the same expertise.
There are a few more on here, like great skills, which we talked about, how bots can learn from you, and then auto-optimization routines.
But I think the last one is also pretty important.
Having a bot that just exists to optimize your other bots.
That means better routines, figuring out where they went wrong or where you had to ask multiple times for something, and then making sure that doesn't happen again.
And overseeing how the other ones work and improving them is way easier than having to do a lot of this yourself.
So save yourself the time.
You set it once, and then you forget it, which is really nice, and I don't see enough people doing that.

Okay, so the first thing is right now we have this feature that you all can use that is, like, putting bots in a group chat.
And that comes with pros and cons.
The pros are that you get a bunch of bots that are really eager to do a lot of work for you and they can work together, which is cool for a very complicated task for you to loop them all in.
The con is that they will all talk.
They all love to talk.

And so I think the part to be cautious about is whether that's the right form factor, right?
Sometimes you just want a bot to tag two other bots once and work with it separately versus having them all in a group chat and watching them all respond like crazy.
So I think that's one that you probably want to stay away from for now.
